head-2048 proxy (79.9\\%) reported.\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, all four models achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all seeds: Qwen-7B (99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00, 3 seeds), Qwen-1.5B (99.83\\%), CodeLlama-13B (99.89\\%$\\pm$0.10, 3 seeds; seed 123 achieves 100\\%), and DeepSeek-6.7B (99.39\\%$\\pm$0.10, 3 seeds).\nThe cross-model range is 0.50pp (99.39--99.89\\%), the tightest of any condition.\n\n\\paragraph{Exception signals across model scales.}\nTier~1 signals are scale-invariant across 1.5B--13B (8.7$\\times$): \\texttt{exception\\_only} spans 0.50pp (99.33--99.89\\%) and \\texttt{ae\\_only} 1.18pp (98.74--99.92\\%).\nCodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\\%, confirming the 2 persistent errors (\\texttt{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts; 99.83\\% is a floor, not a ceiling.\nScale effects concentrate in Tier~2: \\texttt{loop\\_only} spans 8.57pp (77.06--85.63\\%), with CodeLlama-13B below Qwen-1.5B, suggesting architecture matters more than parameter count for lower-density signals.\nIn contrast, \\texttt{no\\_trace} scales with capacity (72.35--76.47\\%, Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_scale}) and shows the highest seed variance ($\\sigma{=}$4.48pp).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\\ model scale. \\texttt{exception\\_only} maintains ${\\geq}$99.33\\% regardless of scale, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} shows monotonic sensitivity (+4.12pp). \\texttt{nomarker} (markers removed) shows no scale degradation.}\n\\label{fig:cross_scale}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{VOTA cross-family variation.}\nExcluding outlier seeds, VOTA achieves ${\\geq}$98.57\\% across all four models (0.80pp range).\nDeepSeek seed 42 (84.45\\%) is a tokenizer-driven truncation failure (91.7\\% of samples exceed 2{,}048 tokens after tokenization; Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), demonstrating that VOTA's longer sequences are sensitive to tokenizer efficiency: 1/4 models suffers catastrophic truncation under standard training length. \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s compact inputs (median 150 tokens) sidestep this vulnerability entirely.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nMulti-seed \\texttt{ae\\_only} ablations use Qwen-7B; \\texttt{loop\\_only} is evaluated across three models to test Tier~2 generalization (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy validated across all four models and three architecture families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}): Tier~1 (${\\geq}$98.53\\%) and Tier~2 (77--86\\%) are separated by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. Entries marked $^\\dag$/$^\\S$/$^\\ddag$ are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; unmarked entries are single seed (seed 42). Per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}. Seed $\\sigma$ negatively correlates with density (0.00 for exception to 0.52 for loop).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & \\textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.44{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.08} & --- & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\\\\nZero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} = 99.41\\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.\nThe Tier~1/2 boundary is unambiguous: the lowest Tier~1 condition (\\texttt{ae\\_only} on DeepSeek, 98.53\\%$\\pm$0.30, 2 seeds) exceeds the highest Tier~2 (\\texttt{loop\\_only} on Qwen-7B, 86.05\\%, CI [83.97, 87.96]) by 12.48\\,pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.\nTier~2 \\texttt{loop\\_only} lifts over \\texttt{no\\_trace} are model-dependent: Qwen shows +10.92pp (7B) and +7.19pp (1.5B, $p{<}.0001$), with the larger 7B gain likely reflecting greater capacity to exploit structured loop patterns; DeepSeek +6.95pp (80.31\\%), CodeLlama +0.93pp (77.40\\%, n.s.).\n\\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}\nThe strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}.001$).\nThe gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \\texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nAcross all formats, SFT outperforms the best applicable regex by +3.62--23.86pp (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}\nZero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\\% across four models (42.44pp range); three-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\\%) improves only 1.76pp.\nSFT compresses this range to 0.50pp (99.39--99.89\\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\\paragraph{Frontier model crossover.}\nTwo frontier models confirm that SFT's advantage depends on input density.\nGPT-4o zero-shot achieves 87.98\\% on \\texttt{exception\\_only} and 84.45\\% on \\texttt{no\\_trace}; GPT-4.1 achieves 82.18\\% and 77.31\\%, respectively.\nUnder \\texttt{no\\_trace}, both frontier models outperform SFT Qwen-7B (75.13\\%) by +9.32pp (GPT-4o) and +2.18pp (GPT-4.1).\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, the ranking reverses: SFT Qwen-7B (99.83\\%) exceeds GPT-4o by 11.85pp and GPT-4.1 by 17.65pp.\nThis crossover shows that compact diagnostic input unlocks SFT's full advantage, while absent or verbose traces favor general reasoning capacity.\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format includes a \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nTo quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\\texttt{nomarker} condition).\n\nQwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\\% $\\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%).\nQwen-1.5B: 97.82\\%, drop of 2.01pp.\nThe marker contribution (${\\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).\nRegex baselines confirm this decomposition: B5 (\\texttt{[No exception data]}$\\to$pass, 99.33\\% with markers) collapses to 28.49\\% without markers, confirming it is entirely format-dependent.\nThe strongest semantic regex B4 (exception class matching, 93.61\\%) is marker-invariant; SFT \\texttt{nomarker} (97.65\\%) exceeds it by 4.04pp, isolating the learned semantic contribution beyond pattern matching.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Decomposition of the Full-Trace Gap}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 17.61pp gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.44\\%$\\pm$1.08, 3-seed mean) and VOTA (99.05\\%, 3-seed mean) can be probed via \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only} (85.94\\%$\\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Preliminary decomposition of the full-trace gap (Qwen-7B, 3-seed means). Label-only loss provides an upper-bound estimate of gradient dilution (${\\leq}$26\\%); the ablation simultaneously changes gradient selection, regularization, and signal distribution.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.44{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.08} & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.50pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.61pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nLabel-only loss improves accuracy by +4.50pp (3-seed mean), providing an upper-bound estimate that gradient dilution accounts for at most ${\\sim}$26\\% of the gap.\nHowever, this ablation simultaneously changes which tokens receive gradients, removes implicit regularization from trace prediction, and alters the training signal distribution; the +4.50pp may partially reflect these confounds rather than gradient dilution alone.\nThe remaining ${\\sim}$74\\% (+13.11pp) reflects information overload at inference: the model receives thousands of irrelevant trace lines that compete for attention with the diagnostic signal, regardless of loss design.\nCross-model evidence supports this: the tail-truncation sharp performance jump at ${\\sim}$500 tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}, all four models) crosses the globals module dump threshold, exposing structured execution markers (\\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values).\nCross-scale validation on Qwen-1.5B yields a consistent pattern (17\\% gradient dilution, 83\\% information overload, 3 seeds), confirming that information overload dominates across model scales.\nCodeLlama-13B and DeepSeek-6.7B both exhibit label-only collapse (28.5\\% accuracy, collapsing to the minority class), limiting the decomposition to models whose tokenizers preserve sufficient trace structure under the training sequence length.\nError analysis provides direct evidence: all 217 \\texttt{full\\_trace} errors occur in samples where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, and among the 61 samples where \\texttt{full\\_trace} underperforms \\texttt{no\\_trace}, all exhibit the same truncation pattern, confirming that information overload manifests through a mechanical truncation pathway rather than semantic confusion.\nThe 26\\%/74\\% attribution is a preliminary upper-bound estimate limited to the Qwen family (2 scales, 3 seeds each); the two factors may interact, and label-only loss changes multiple optimization dynamics simultaneously.\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\\%, $p{=}.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).\n\n\\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).\nAll four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.\n\nA cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a sharp performance jump localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.\nAt tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--80.7\\%, only +1.0--4.8pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\\to$512 (+13.2--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\\sim}$94--98\\%).\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens, crossing the globals module dump threshold (${\\sim}$384 tokens) and exposing structured execution markers (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).\nCodeLlama tail conditions use label-only loss (\\S\\ref{sec:density}); cross-model tail comparisons should account for this training difference.\nBeyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%] (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\n\\texttt{tail\\_2048} captures 95--98\\% of \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\nThe residual 2--5pp gap confirms that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\texttt{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:calibration}).\nThis calibration advantage generalizes across architectures and scales: all four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.005 under \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration_model_level} in Appendix), indicating well-calibrated predictions rather than overfit confidence.\n\n\\paragraph{Error analysis.}\n\\texttt{exception\\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000; Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_overlap}); per-type breakdown confirms all standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy, with only \\texttt{GeneratorExit} (a benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclass) causing failures (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\n\n\\paragraph{Length control.}\nPadding \\texttt{exception\\_only} to 2{,}048 tokens, even in a semantically loaded position (after the verdict region), yields identical accuracy (99.83\\%), confirming the 18+pp gap arises from spurious patterns in verbose trace content, not sequence length.\n\n\\paragraph{Supplementary OOD analysis.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nOn DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the condition ranking is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\texttt{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%.\nHowever, narrow margins (3.29pp headroom from \\texttt{no\\_trace}) limit discriminative power; this supplementary analysis confirms the ranking but cannot establish effect sizes comparable to the primary dataset (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n",

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	
3	\subsection{Task Definition and Diagnostic Signal Metrics}
4	
5	We study \emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.
6	The model produces a single token (\texttt{pass} or \texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.
7	Unlike code generation \citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks.
8	
9	To quantify signal quality without circular reference to model accuracy, we define the \emph{diagnostic token ratio} $d = n_{\text{diag}} / n_{\text{total}}$, where $n_{\text{diag}}$ counts tokens matching exception or assertion patterns.
10	This model-free metric correlates with accuracy at Pearson $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$, $N{=}5$ main conditions), but captures only the \emph{explicit} diagnostic pathway; tail-truncated conditions achieve 93--97\% with $d {\approx} 0$, indicating a second, implicit signal channel operating through terminal execution state (\S\ref{sec:density}).
11	
12	\subsection{Dataset}
13	\label{sec:dataset}
14	
15	We sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\% pass, 33\% fail).
16	The dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\%).
17	The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\% train, 48.99\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).
18	
19	\subsection{Trace Representations}
20	
21	We define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\ref{fig:trace_overview}):
22	\begin{itemize}
23	\item \texttt{no\_trace}: Source code and test input only.
24	\item \texttt{full\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.
25	\item \texttt{VOTA} (Verdict-Optimized Trace Abstraction): Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression (Appendix~\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).
26	\item \texttt{exception\_only}: Only exception/error events.
27	\item \texttt{ae\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.
28	\item \texttt{loop\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.
29	\item \texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.
30	\end{itemize}
31	
32	\begin{figure*}[t]
33	\centering
34	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}
35	\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \texttt{no\_trace} (75.13\%), \texttt{full\_trace} (81.44\%), VOTA (99.05\%), and \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$); multi-seed means where available (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}). Appendix~\ref{sec:vota_examples} provides input/output examples.}
36	\label{fig:trace_overview}
37	\end{figure*}
38	
39	\subsection{Models and Training}
40	
41	We evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \citep{qwen2024coder}, \textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \citep{roziere2024code}, and \textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\times$).
42	All conditions use LoRA \citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.
43	Sequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.
44	The primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \texttt{exception\_only} robustness (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_details}).
45	
46	\subsection{Loss Ablation}
47	
48	To probe the contribution of gradient dilution, we introduce \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).
49	TRL's SFTTrainer computes mean cross-entropy over label tokens, so label-only loss does not amplify the effective learning rate.
50	However, this ablation simultaneously changes three variables: (1)~which tokens receive gradient updates, (2)~the implicit regularization from predicting trace tokens, and (3)~the training signal distribution.
51	The resulting improvement over standard \texttt{full\_trace} provides an \emph{upper-bound estimate} of gradient dilution's contribution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects information overload at inference, but the decomposition is approximate (\S\ref{sec:density}).
52	
```

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/method.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition and Diagnostic Signal Metrics}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\texttt{pass} or \\texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks.\n\nTo quantify signal quality without circular reference to model accuracy, we define the \\emph{diagnostic token ratio} $d = n_{\\text{diag}} / n_{\\text{total}}$, where $n_{\\text{diag}}$ counts tokens matching exception or assertion patterns.\nThis model-free metric correlates with accuracy at Pearson $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$, $N{=}5$ main conditions), but captures only the \\emph{explicit} diagnostic pathway; tail-truncated conditions achieve 93--97\\% with $d {\\approx} 0$, indicating a second, implicit signal channel operating through terminal execution state (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\texttt{no\\_trace}: Source code and test input only.\n\\item \\texttt{full\\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA} (Verdict-Optimized Trace Abstraction): Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\n\\item \\texttt{exception\\_only}: Only exception/error events.\n\\item \\texttt{ae\\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.\n\\item \\texttt{loop\\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\n\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.44\\%), VOTA (99.05\\%), and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$); multi-seed means where available (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}). Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_examples} provides input/output examples.}\n\\label{fig:trace_overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2024coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\texttt{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo probe the contribution of gradient dilution, we introduce \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nTRL's SFTTrainer computes mean cross-entropy over label tokens, so label-only loss does not amplify the effective learning rate.\nHowever, this ablation simultaneously changes three variables: (1)~which tokens receive gradient updates, (2)~the implicit regularization from predicting trace tokens, and (3)~the training signal distribution.\nThe resulting improvement over standard \\texttt{full\\_trace} provides an \\emph{upper-bound estimate} of gradient dilution's contribution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects information overload at inference, but the decomposition is approximate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n",
      "numLines": 52,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 52
    }
  },
